# Daniel Iles — The A–Z Playbook (with G&L M&A Advisory applications)

Distilled from **90 of the 92 long-form videos** on Daniel Iles' YouTube channel (≈32 hours, every transcript read in full, October 2026). Daniel built Viral Coach to ~$5M/month selling a high-ticket B2B program through Meta ads → landing page/VSL → application → sales call, with a large commission sales team.

Every rule links to the exact moment in the video where he says it. Where his advice changed over time or contradicts itself, the rule says so. Each topic ends with **For G&L**: concrete moves for G&L M&A Advisory (buy-side: acquiring with little money down via vendor finance; sell-side: getting owners 55+ exit-ready and sold). Many of Daniel's points on profit, owner-independence and systems also double as **advice G&L can give its clients** (marked *Seller advice* / *Buyer advice*).

Companion files: [video-notes.md](video-notes.md) (notes on every video) · [video-index.md](video-index.md) (all 92 videos) · full verbatim transcripts: run `tools/daniel-iles/fetch_transcripts.sh`.

## The 30 rules that matter most

**Offer & price**
1. **Suspect the offer first.** If cold traffic won't buy, no ad, page or script fixes it. Test the offer on cold traffic before you scale anything. → [A1](#a1-offer-design)
2. **Model a proven offer; don't invent one.** He copied a working ads → call → high-ticket program model and bought the knowledge rather than discovering it. → [A1](#a1-offer-design), [F6](#f6-mindset--decision-making-buying-experience-focus-simplicity-boring-businesses-risk)
3. **One offer, one price, per audience.** Removing the cheaper option raised revenue per lead ~40%. → [A1](#a1-offer-design)
4. **Price is the biggest lever.** Test in big jumps. A 30–40% close rate means you're too cheap; 60% means double the price. → [A2](#a2-pricing--payment-terms)
5. **Collect upfront and sell an outcome, not a subscription.** Switching from weekly billing to a paid-upfront fixed term took him from $1.3M to $3M/month in 30 days. → [A2](#a2-pricing--payment-terms), [E2](#e2-finance-cash-flow--debt)
6. **Set the contract term just above how long happy clients stay** (~5 months → 6-month minimum). → [A2](#a2-pricing--payment-terms)
7. **Cold traffic needs a believable outcome guarantee**, designed from your own results data so that it makes you money. Believability beats size. → [A3](#a3-guarantees--trials)
8. **Fulfillment is the real ceiling.** Before scaling, ask "could we double sales next month and still deliver?" → [A4](#a4-fulfillment-delivery--client-results)
9. **Proof is the most valuable asset.** Capture wins continuously and package each one as an ad, an email, a call slide and a page section. → [A5](#a5-case-studies--proof)

**Traffic**
10. **Optimise for revenue, not cheap leads.** The cheapest calls are often non-buyers; chasing them nearly bankrupted him. Judge ads on cost per qualified call (SQL) and ROAS. → [B1](#b1-paid-ads-meta-readiness-economics-structure-testing-scaling-benchmarks), [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
11. **Derive targets from your own LTV:** CAC ≤ 30% of LTV → work back to cost per booked call. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
12. **Creative volume wins:** about one good new video per $5K of monthly spend; ≥100 creatives before you call it a test. Test angles before hooks. → [B2](#b2-creative-hooks-scripts-angles-formats-volume-testing-cadence-and-the-content-flywheel)
13. **Keep ad spend ≤ ~25% of revenue.** Above that, pull back testing and feed proven winners. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
14. **Attribute every sale to its source.** Run separate, identical funnels for paid, organic and email. Blended numbers hid that his ads were losing money. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track), [F8](#f8-his-personal-story-failures--lessons)
15. **Organic closes ~3× better than paid.** Use content to warm and filter, and paid to scale. → [B4](#b4-organic-content-vs-paid-how-they-work-together)

**Funnel**
16. **Put a dollar value on every application answer** and route the best leads to the best reps. Revenue band, email domain and booking lead time each swing revenue per call by 250–700%. → [C5](#c5-application--qualification-forms-and-lead-scoring)
17. **Speed to lead under 5 minutes, and engage before the call.** Prospects who opened the email *and* replied to a text show and qualify ~42% of the time, against ~3% for those who did neither. → [C6](#c6-appointment-setting), [C7](#c7-show-up-rates--pre-call-nurture)
18. **Book 2–7 days out**, with a reminder stack and a named rep texting. → [C7](#c7-show-up-rates--pre-call-nurture)
19. **The VSL/page must state what you do, why it's different, proof and the CTA in the first ~3 minutes**, and say who it's *not* for. → [C2](#c2-vsls-structure-length-and-the-vsls-are-dead-view)

**Sales**
20. **Revenue per lead (RPL) is the master KPI.** Rank reps on RPL, not close rate. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track), [D4](#d4-managing-a-sales-team-kpis-close-rates-cash-collected-coaching-call-reviews-culture-rep-tiers)
21. **The founder closes first and longer than feels comfortable.** He thinks he should have stayed on the phones a full year. → [D1](#d1-the-sales-call-and-script-structure-stages-discovery-pitch-close)
22. **Handle objections before they appear.** Ask about authority (spouse/partner) up front, isolate money ("money aside, is this right for you?"), and never cut the price. → [D2](#d2-objection-handling-timing-money-spouse-think-about-it-etc)
23. **Hire proven closers, tier the leads, and pay on cash collected.** New reps start on the lowest-tier leads; sales comp runs ~5–6% of revenue all-in. → [D3](#d3-hiring-sales-reps-profile-sourcing-screening-comp-onboarding)
24. **Make operational drag visible:** (best rep's RPL − this rep's RPL) × leads = dollars lost. Keep closers at 70–80% capacity and no more than 5 calls in a row. → [D4](#d4-managing-a-sales-team-kpis-close-rates-cash-collected-coaching-call-reviews-culture-rep-tiers), [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)

**Money, team, founder**
25. **Be cash-on-cash profitable every 30 days.** Keep a reserve, and never fund ads with unsecured debt (he hit $500K of card debt and $400 in the bank). → [E2](#e2-finance-cash-flow--debt)
26. **Take ~10% of topline as profit and reinvest the rest** until you hit your target. → [E2](#e2-finance-cash-flow--debt)
27. **Forecast from unit economics** (spend ÷ CPL × RPL) and compare the forecast with the actual every month. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
28. **Organise into Get / Keep / Jail:** one person gets clients, one keeps them, one keeps the other two in line. Labour cost per revenue dollar should *fall* as you scale. → [F1](#f1-team-building--org-structure-incl-a-100-person-team-without-killing-profit), [F4](#f4-operations-systems--sops)
29. **Set the KPIs for every seat before you hire for it**, and never let a senior hire define their own. → [F2](#f2-hiring-non-sales-roles), [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
30. **Buy experience.** Each big jump ($500K→$1M, $1.3M→$3M, $3M→$5M) came from one outside insight, not from more hours. Keep the business simple and "boring". → [F6](#f6-mindset--decision-making-buying-experience-focus-simplicity-boring-businesses-risk)

## G&L M&A Advisory: the action plan

A consolidated sequence drawn from the *For G&L* blocks throughout this playbook. Numbers in *italics* are illustrations; replace them with G&L's own data.

### Phase 1 (weeks 0–4): offer, proof, measurement
1. **Two offers, two funnels.** *Buyer:* "acquire a cash-flowing business with little money down using seller finance." *Seller (55+):* "get exit-ready and sold for a premium, on your timeline." Each gets its own ads, page, application, pixel, calendar and VSL. Don't merge them until each is proven. → [C1](#c1-funnel-architecture)
2. **One core engagement per side at one price,** paid upfront with a payment ladder (pay-in-full discount → split → deposit + monthly). Set the term just past how long a typical engagement takes to show a result. → [A2](#a2-pricing--payment-terms)
3. **Design the guarantee from data.** Promise a believable intermediate result G&L controls (e.g. *X vetted, seller-finance-ready deals presented* or *an exit-readiness score uplift and buyer-ready CIM*). Condition it on the client doing the work. Keep claims compliant. → [A3](#a3-guarantees--trials)
4. **Build the proof bank before ads.** Make anonymised deal tombstones (industry, price, % down, % vendor-financed) and owner exit stories (multiple achieved vs. first valuation). Package each as an ad, an email, a call slide and a page block. → [A5](#a5-case-studies--proof)
5. **Instrument the business from day one.** Set up UTMs and separate funnels per source, a per-rep sheet (leads, show %, qualified %, close %, **RPL**, cash collected in 30 days, refunds) and an SQL definition. Buyer SQL: liquid capital above your floor, operating experience, a timeline under 90 days. Seller SQL: EBITDA above your floor, an exit window under 24 months. → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track), [D4](#d4-managing-a-sales-team-kpis-close-rates-cash-collected-coaching-call-reviews-culture-rep-tiers)
6. **Set targets from LTV.** CAC ≤ 30% of first-30-day cash per client → target cost per booked call, worked out separately for buyers and sellers. → [B1](#b1-paid-ads-meta-readiness-economics-structure-testing-scaling-benchmarks)

### Phase 2 (weeks 4–8): launch traffic
7. **Applications that score.** Buyer form: capital bracket, past spend on courses and advisors, target size, operating experience, partner or spouse involvement, start date. Seller form: revenue and EBITDA band, years owned, age band, exit timeline, owner-dependence, other decision-makers. Put a dollar value on every answer and route on it. → [C5](#c5-application--qualification-forms-and-lead-scoring)
8. **Meta at $10–15K/month to start:** ABO, one ad per ad set, test each ad to 3–5× KPI, fire the pixel only on qualified thank-you pages, use a rolling 3-day review, and allow longer judging windows for sellers. → [B1](#b1-paid-ads-meta-readiness-economics-structure-testing-scaling-benchmarks)
9. **Get to 100 creatives in 60 days.** Founder talking heads on a 2×5 angle matrix (e.g. "the seller is the bank", "no successor? exit in 24 months"), notes-app statics, Loom deal walkthroughs and interviews with exited owners. Take hooks verbatim from recorded calls. → [B2](#b2-creative-hooks-scripts-angles-formats-volume-testing-cadence-and-the-content-flywheel)
10. **Compliance:** no personal-attribute callouts ("Are you over 55?"), and keep "no money down" and return claims out of ad headlines. → [B1](#b1-paid-ads-meta-readiness-economics-structure-testing-scaling-benchmarks)
11. **Organic in parallel:** a *Deal Breakdowns* series (buyers) and an *Exit Ready* series (owners) on YouTube and Facebook, LinkedIn posts from advisors, and ManyChat keywords ("DEAL", "EXIT"). Retarget viewers. → [B4](#b4-organic-content-vs-paid-how-they-work-together)

### Phase 3 (weeks 6–12): conversion engine
12. **Speed and show-up:** reach every booking within 5 minutes (named-advisor iMessages + coordinator call). Book 1–4 days out. Send a formal, light reminder stack to 55+ sellers. Send a pre-call pack: a seller-finance explainer and tombstones for buyers; "what buyers look for" and a sample valuation for sellers. → [C6](#c6-appointment-setting), [C7](#c7-show-up-rates--pre-call-nurture)
13. **Two call flowcharts** (acquirer and seller) covering authority, strategy so far, dream outcome, finances asked indirectly, numbers and timing. Open with homework on their business or LinkedIn. Put the cost of waiting in numbers. Collect the deposit and e-signature and book onboarding on the call. → [D1](#d1-the-sales-call-and-script-structure-stages-discovery-pitch-close)
14. **Objection playbook:** "I don't have the capital" → "you have a deal-structure problem, not a capital problem". "My accountant…" → bring the accountant onto the call or into onboarding. "Not ready for 2–3 years" → "use the next 12 months to get exit-ready so the timeline is yours". Tag every lost call by budget, authority, need or timing, and turn the top loss reason into VSL and ad content each quarter. → [D2](#d2-objection-handling-timing-money-spouse-think-about-it-etc)
15. **Founders close until at least ~$250–500K/month,** then hire experienced closers with financial fluency and pay them on cash collected (with refund clawback). Use separate buyer and seller seats, and start new reps on tier-3 leads. → [D3](#d3-hiring-sales-reps-profile-sourcing-screening-comp-onboarding)

### Phase 4 (month 3+): scale without breaking delivery
16. **Gate ad spend on advisor capacity.** Use a headcount-from-revenue sheet: calls → closers at 70–80% → advisors per active client → ops support, plus a buffer. → [F1](#f1-team-building--org-structure-incl-a-100-person-team-without-killing-profit)
17. **Get / Keep / Jail:** ads and sales / deal advisors / contracts, compliance and finance, with one owner per bucket and no overlap between the two founders. Write a founders' agreement with a deadlock rule. → [F1](#f1-team-building--org-structure-incl-a-100-person-team-without-killing-profit), [F5](#f5-partnerships-co-founders-friends-spouse)
18. **Buy M&A experience** (fractional M&A lawyer, QoE accountant, broker) instead of learning on client deals. Write SOPs for buyer onboarding, deal screening, term sheets, the exit-readiness audit and data-room setup. → [F2](#f2-hiring-non-sales-roles), [F4](#f4-operations-systems--sops)
19. **Cash discipline:** cash-on-cash profitable within 30 days before raising spend, a written risk budget, a reserve floor, and no personal credit cards funding ads. → [E2](#e2-finance-cash-flow--debt)

### Daniel's lessons G&L can sell to its clients
- **Seller advice: owner-independence is valuation.** If the owner *is* the get or keep function, buyers discount it as key-person risk. Hire the GM 12–24 months before sale, document SOPs and show a period the business ran without the owner. → [F1](#f1-team-building--org-structure-incl-a-100-person-team-without-killing-profit), [F4](#f4-operations-systems--sops)
- **Seller advice: profit, not revenue, sets the price.** Fix pricing and margins first (what PE fixes first), collect upfront, track revenue by source, and reduce customer and platform concentration. → [E3](#e3-profit-margins--the-profit-levers), [F8](#f8-his-personal-story-failures--lessons)
- **Seller advice: clean numbers shorten diligence.** Prepare a KPI pack (revenue per customer, gross margin, CAC:LTV, churn, concentration). → [E1](#e1-kpis--every-number-to-track)
- **Buyer advice: buy boring, uncapped businesses.** Screen targets for Daniel's three caps (location, key person, platform) and ask "can revenue be bought with ads or outbound?" → [F6](#f6-mindset--decision-making-buying-experience-focus-simplicity-boring-businesses-risk), [F8](#f8-his-personal-story-failures--lessons)
- **Buyer advice: co-buyers need a written agreement** with non-overlapping roles and a buy-sell clause before the LOI. → [F5](#f5-partnerships-co-founders-friends-spouse)

<a id="a-offer"></a>

## A. Offer, Pricing, Guarantees, Fulfillment & Proof

### A1. Offer design

1. **Suspect the offer first.** Warning signs: under $100K/month while selling several offers; it doesn't sell to cold traffic on ads; you can only close in person; doubling fulfillment would break it. One good offer in a big enough market should reach $100K/month. When ads fail, the cause is almost always the offer, because what converts warm referrals rarely converts strangers. When everything feels broken, it's usually an offer problem. ([$36K training 1:55:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=6937s); [Meta Ads Blueprint 1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eEiyrvM1nB8&t=92s); [$10K vs $1M ads 14:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=891s))
2. **Run the cold-traffic test before you buy ads.** Ask whether you could sell it to a stranger on the street. E-com sells itself on the checkout page; a lawyer selling "you" has to prove he's better. Check revenue mix: if 90% comes from organic, the offer only works warm; if 99% comes from paid despite a big organic audience, it sells cold. His organic-converting offer failed on paid, costing about $500K of debt and about 6 months rebuilding it. ([Organic vs paid 4:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Hk0k66Pc5Dk&t=246s); [Lessons to $3M 27:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=1624s); [Organic vs paid 3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Hk0k66Pc5Dk&t=184s))
3. **Productize for cold, personalize for warm.** If you can say what the client gets in three words or less, it's a product and can sell cold. Services are intangible and need trust. Viral Coach sells "X views / we post videos for you" to cold traffic and "work with Daniel's team" to warm traffic, with identical delivery. "Work with me, I'll help you" means nothing to a stranger scrolling, so the cold offer has to stand without your personal brand. ([$36K training 1:57:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=7028s); [$100K vs $1M offers 3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=183s); [Lessons to $3M 17:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=1073s))
4. **Anatomy of a cold-traffic offer:** easy to understand (a one-page contract), risk reversal, built around a one-call close, and everything stated up front so nothing new surprises the client later. Cold traffic needs less complexity in the sales process, the engagement and the delivery. If your marketing depends on a VSL, the offer can't be described simply. The jump to $4M/month came from fixing the offer, not from a funnel tweak worth about 20% in conversion ("car dealership": people may dislike the sale and still spend $50K happily). ([$10K vs $1M ads 15:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=953s); [Selling cold traffic 11:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=681s); [VSLs are dead 5:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AATxUdXv4tI&t=305s))
5. **Desirability before quality.** The most desirable offer makes the most money, not the best one. His first version (1-on-1 with him, tens of thousands a month) got great results but sold badly because prospects didn't believe them. A cheaper, more accessible front end let him grow. Sequence: make it desirable → model what works → add your edge → launch → only then optimize for profit. Launch fast and improve using your first customers. ([$36K training 2:04:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=7464s); [$36K training 2:12:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=7954s))
6. **Model proven offers; don't invent.** Two routes: copy a winning competitor and differentiate on brand and trust, since buyers can't compare delivery before buying; or take a proven offer to a new demographic. "Originality on cold traffic is extremely expensive," so stop endlessly refining. Model competitors doing $1–10M/month a few steps ahead of you, but first check they're actually profitable (he once copied coaches with leased Lamborghinis who turned out to be broke). Hire closers from the biggest competitor and ask exactly what that competitor pitched. ([$36K training 2:09:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=7769s); [Wife Q&A 19:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_j_83cglBA&t=1180s); [1M/mo lesson 2:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y8WB72fKX1Q&t=154s))
7. **But copying the surface fails.** Word-for-word copies of ads, funnel and offer rarely work, because the moat is the invisible machine: spend, closers doing $1M+/month, and fulfillment. A copycat's 7-day refund guarantee lasted about two weeks. Competitors can copy the ads but not the contract structure, pricing and customer insight behind them. ([Message to competitors 0:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t30Xo5uFkGk&t=32s); [Lessons to $3M 1:13:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=4438s))
8. **Market and offer are one decision, so pick buyers who already pay.** Coaches and consultants were mostly broke. Switching to traditional businesses and professional services that already spend on marketing doubled revenue monthly for six months ($80K → $160K → ~$300K → ~$600K → $1.2M). The pitch became reallocating existing budget, not spending new money. Let lead data move the target market, and choose a market where you control the lead flow; he left influencer work because brand budgets were feast-or-famine. ([Lessons to $3M 0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=0s); [5 lessons 3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J0YiARB-zXY&t=184s); [Why I disappeared 9:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KeU8LhMBdbA&t=583s))
9. **Validate by moving proven demand down-market.** Fortune 50 brands paid him $10–20K per 30-second video for years. He packaged the same capability for $1–50M-revenue businesses, where it doubled or tripled their marketing efficiency. Saturated markets give you many companies to model; avoid anything completely untested. ([Lessons to $3M 3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=217s); [Why I disappeared 1:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KeU8LhMBdbA&t=114s); [Lessons to $3M 7:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=462s))
10. **Narrow until you are first.** Being second is worthless. Pick the "drawer" where you rank first; the alternative to buying every competitor is narrowing the market and service until you're the only one. Example definition: social media marketing for traditional business owners with $1M+ revenue who need short-form content and have only a few hours a month. Then expand vertically with vertical-specific case studies before claiming the broad category. Sell new things under a label buyers already trust ("a marketing company that specialises in social media", not "a social media company"). Only enter markets where you have an inherent advantage, and fight a leader on a different channel. ([Best version overnight 1:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=82s); [5 lessons 4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J0YiARB-zXY&t=245s); [Easy businesses win 8:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i4mWmsC0mTA&t=493s))
11. **Pitch the mechanism at your market's level of sophistication.** Theirs is just "we film you, the same way you found me through a video ad." Over-engineered mechanisms confuse buyers; the mechanism exists to help the buyer justify the purchase. The #1 cold-call objection is "I can't picture how this works for me," so tell it as First → Next → Then. Long-form version: (1) identify the bottleneck, (2) build the system, (3) launch, test and adjust in the first weeks, (4) guarantee growth with a full written refund. Make "how it works" a run of objection answers (custom strategy → not cookie-cutter; a staffed editor → "no time"). ([Raw $5M convo 30:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=1836s); [Meta Ads Blueprint 21:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eEiyrvM1nB8&t=1264s); [Content flywheel 14:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xvOnEA48xZY&t=897s))
12. **Build the offer one objection at a time.** The founder runs sales early, logs every objection and changes the offer so it never comes up again. Viral Coach went scripts → editing ("I need an editor first") → studio setup → social profiles → websites, and each addition opened a colder audience and justified a higher price. "Burned by agencies" was answered with anti-agency positioning: set up the system, hand it over, help hire the in-house editor, and charge more upfront to cover early exits. A "you hire us, then you fire us" ad closed a $30K paid-in-full deal in its first week. When a prospect says exactly what must happen before they'd buy, treat it as offer research. ([$100K vs $1M offers 12:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=762s); [$100K vs $1M offers 18:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=1099s); [Employee ad contest 16:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fFmGhsryRiI&t=992s))
13. **One offer, one price, per audience, on the call.** Two products, one 6× the other's price, confused prospects (who bought neither) and reps. Removing the cheaper one lifted revenue per lead 40% immediately and took them from $1.4M to $3M the next month. That $6K offer was about 80% of customers but 20% of revenue, and purchase counts barely moved after the cut. A client of Ryan's dropped a $3K offer and saw ROAS jump about 50%, doubling within weeks. Removing the downsell on 40-minute Zoom calls didn't change the number of sales; everyone simply bought at the higher price. Three offers with five sub-tiers caused decision fatigue ("what do you think we should do?"). Customise after the sale; the only price variant is financing. To test it, have one rep pitch only the single offer and compare revenue per lead. ([Harsh lessons 3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9XPfyJpWA6A&t=183s); [Iman Gadzhi call 6:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ocPw7KvY710&t=373s); [Raw $5M convo 38:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2325s))
14. **Keep the business simple.** $3M/month ran on two near-identical packages, and he removed one of three deliverables to simplify. Five to ten packages below $1M/month is a headache. Don't run incongruent offers side by side (a $30K production service next to a $50/month LinkedIn school). Doing one thing that sells about a thousand times a month beats chasing one $20K custom client. ([$3M in one month 15:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uci8AF7uU9A&t=959s); [Roasted owner 3:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cZQ4hJNpW7o&t=186s); [Boring $1.5M 7:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ggukLlqtJ7s&t=430s))
15. **Ladders, downsells and front ends: his view is mixed.** For: *5 Frameworks* recommends a premium tier at 5–10× the core once you're at about $200K/month (he has seen businesses double revenue with an offer about 6× core), plus a downsell 5–10× below core. Low-ticket brings only 10–20% of revenue but produces reviews, case studies and an upsell pool. His $1M/month inner circle sits on a $5K/month "half circle" waitlist and a ~$1K/month AI product selling 5–20 a day as feeders, and the premium tier itself grew from an above-average segment inside a cheaper offer. A realistic premium anchor at 100× price doubles revenue at only 1% uptake. Against: he resisted a low-ticket "sample offer" (new products are never hands-off) and only launches something new after 3 months of flat income. The reconciliation he lands on: never a menu on the call. Low-price front ends work only with built-in ascension (e.g. a $2K auto-webinar, then "this is $30K, pay $24K now" on the onboarding call) or with silent application routing of non-fits to free content or a low-ticket community. ([5 Frameworks 4:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=d-e18KypwBQ&t=277s); [$3M in one month 17:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uci8AF7uU9A&t=1022s); [Scaling to $10M 1:18:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=4684s))
16. **Move toward done-for-you as CAC rises.** A cheap DIY price is the buyer's ceiling, not their preference. Cold-traffic $7 ebooks died about a decade ago and $2–3K courses about 5 years ago, and $7–8K programs now struggle. DFY at $20–50K+ works because a $3–5K CAC against a ~$40K LTV leaves room. You can't just raise a course's price, though, because moving upmarket changes the buyer, so deliverables and marketing must change too. Hyper-custom delivery only works for enterprise firms (PwC/EY); with cold Facebook ads you must systematize. "Pseudo-DFY" (client participates, you run technical delivery with AI tools trained on them) supports 5–30+ DFY clients. ([DFY vs DIY 2:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XmRo-NwqODk&t=125s); [DFY vs DIY 3:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XmRo-NwqODk&t=219s); [Every revenue level 17:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1055s))
17. **Kill hidden costs; friction grows as traffic gets colder.** A ~$3K gear requirement was fine until ~$500K/month. It was cut to a ~$300 kit, and by ~$1M/month even $300 felt like a scam, so gear now ships free. An $80/month tool drew "upsell scam" complaints at $1.3–1.5M/month; an $8/month Slack fee lost contracts at $3M/month. Bundle required add-ons into one all-in price. ([Selling cold traffic 8:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=495s); [Selling cold traffic 9:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=556s); [Selling cold traffic 10:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=650s))
18. **Repackage before you build.** The biggest late-month win was selling the same thing differently: reframe the promise, shift more of the price upfront, or highlight a component you already deliver. Splitting an included deliverable out as a same-day bonus ("if you're ready / bring your team to the call") roughly doubled revenue per lead. Sell one component as an entry angle ("staff a professional editor"), then the strategy it needs. Sell what the market thinks it wants (views), then show it doesn't produce profit and deliver what they actually want (revenue). Split-test packaging across thumbnails, ad headlines, VSL headlines and the pitch. ([$3M in one month 12:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uci8AF7uU9A&t=771s); [$700K on FB ads 3:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7u3gnQT-IDo&t=218s); [$3M AMA 18:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rO6lGSXn42E&t=1087s))
19. **Earn the back end after the first win.** Sell cold traffic only the narrow thing it asked for. Don't pitch upsells to skeptical new clients, who will ask for refunds; include extras free, then after a proven result propose the paid next step. The back end can earn 3–5× the front end. Prepayment makes maintenance upsells easy: after $30K upfront, $500/week to protect it feels small (like a $30/month monitoring plan after a $3K security system). Ongoing fees fit services only you can provide. ([$100K vs $1M offers 20:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=1220s); [$20K lesson 5:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=304s); [5 Frameworks 3:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=d-e18KypwBQ&t=184s))
20. **Use real scarcity and real reasons to act now.** At $946K recurring he posted the numbers, said six slots were left before a $1M/month cap, and all six filled in under 24 hours. Without a genuine deadline or cap, people buy at random times. ([$1M/mo offer 21:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1299s); [$1M/mo offer 32:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1943s))
21. **Two valid scaling paths; choose deliberately.** Aggressive (ads, sales team, fulfillment) usually needs a concrete, quantified outcome or guarantee in the offer. Lean lifestyle (organic plus cheap software, ~99% margins) doesn't. Going from $5M to $10M means a separate offer for a new market, not just better ads. Before you have a program, sell 30- or 60-minute paid consults and let the questions shape it. ([$1M biz for a stranger 11:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=714s); [Scaling to $10M 36:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=2199s); [5 mistakes $30K 31:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9o5csXRJXeU&t=1863s))
22. **Performance and rev-share models: conflicting views.** A guest (Ryan Clogg) prefers percentage or performance consulting so he never takes $50K from someone who didn't make it back. Daniel says rev-share or equity only makes sense with very large companies; with small businesses it ends in no result or lawsuits. Agencies sell "multiple people's worth of experience from day one," which is the value proposition. ([Iman Gadzhi call 3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ocPw7KvY710&t=217s); [Lessons to $3M 1:22:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=4930s); [Buying XP 7:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=segcbjg2cGs&t=438s))

> "Every part of your offer must overcome the objections you hear during the sale." ([$100K vs $1M offers 12:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=762s))
> "fixing the product, fixing the offer and making it make sense to people who don't trust us inherently" ([VSLs are dead 5:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AATxUdXv4tI&t=335s))
> "That product price point... is not the preference of the buyer — it is literally their price ceiling." ([DFY vs DIY 1:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XmRo-NwqODk&t=94s))

**For G&L:**
- Run two productized offers, one price each, never pitched together: **Buy-side:** "Acquisition Program: buy-box → vetted off-market targets → signed LOI on a vendor-financed deal." **Sell-side:** "Exit-Readiness & Sale Program: valuation baseline → EBITDA/price fixes → buyer search → structured close." Use ≤3-word deliverable names in cold ads ("seller-financed deal", "exit-ready in 9 months"); keep "work with Bendik/Josh's team" for retargeting.
- Write the mechanism as First/Next/Then for each side. Sell-side: First we value and clean up the business; Next we find and vet the buyer; Then we structure the vendor-finance deal and close.
- Turn every logged objection into a deliverable. "I can't find deals" → off-market sourcing. "Banks won't lend / I have no capital" → vendor-finance and lender structuring. "I can't run it alone" → borrow our legal/finance/integration bench. "My business isn't sellable" → a pricing and margin fix (PE-style, before sale) that lifts EBITDA and therefore the multiple.
- Weight the offer toward markets that already pay: owners 55+ with $1–10M revenue who already pay brokers and accountants, and buyers with $50K+ liquid. Route unfunded aspiring buyers from the application to free content or a separate low-ticket product. Never offer it as an on-call downsell.
- Price all-inclusive: fold valuation reports, data room, LOI/legal templates and deal-sourcing tools into the fee, and state third-party costs (lawyers, QoE, lender fees) explicitly on the call.
- Define the category narrowly enough to be first in it, e.g. "vendor-finance acquisition advisory for corporate professionals buying their first $1–5M-EBITDA business" and "exit-readiness for founders 55+ with no successor", not generic "M&A advisory."
- Once there's volume, add a capped premium anchor priced 5–10× core (done-for-you search, negotiation and close for capitalised buyers or $1M+ EBITDA sellers). Add back-end offers only after the first win: post-acquisition board seat, fractional CFO or integration retainer, and seller value-protection.
- Run the cold test monthly: split closed revenue by source (Meta vs referral/organic). If paid is weak, fix the offer before adding closers or spend.

### A2. Pricing & payment terms

1. **Price is the biggest revenue and profit lever.** Ticket price beats lead volume, which beats close rate, and the business sets price, not the rep. Revenue per lead = ticket price × close %. $3K at 50% equals $6K at 25% in gross, with half the clients to serve. A $3K product and a $48K product, 100 leads each, closing 50% vs ~3.1%, give the same gross, but delivery costs $100K vs $6K, so about 3× the profit. ([1.8% close rate 1:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OAtocDg2Mqo&t=60s); [1.8% close rate 6:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OAtocDg2Mqo&t=374s); [1.8% close rate 7:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OAtocDg2Mqo&t=436s))
2. **Know the margin math, and never discount.** At $5K with $3.5K delivery cost, margin is 30%. A 20% price rise (to $6K) lifts profit 67% (42% margin); a 20% cut (to $4K) drops profit 70%. In another example, $3K price, $2K delivery and $500 overhead give 33% gross and 17% net. Taking gross margin from 33% to 50% triples net profit ($500 → $1,500), and a 33% price rise ($3K → $4K) gets there without asking staff to take pay cuts; new customers don't know yesterday's price. Doubling to $6K gives $3,500 net (+700%); even losing half the clients (500 → 250), revenue is unchanged with half the delivery load. ([$36K training 2:14:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=8078s); [Profit-margin math 2:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W0pUXPxvQyI&t=154s); [Profit-margin math 4:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W0pUXPxvQyI&t=247s))
3. **Test prices with big jumps.** Skip value-based pricing. Double the price for a day or a week (depending on sample size) and watch volume. If you lose less than half the buyers, double again; losing exactly half gives the same revenue with half the fulfillment, overhead and admin. 5–10% tweaks drown in noise. He cut $24K to $12K, doubled prices, multiplied one service 6×, and added or removed 3 months of term; within a week you know if you're over- or underpriced, then refine with small moves. For sophisticated B2B buyers, $50 is a throwaway price. Track impressions, clicks and revenue per week at each price. ([Lessons to $3M 22:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=1348s); [$3M AMA 5:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rO6lGSXn42E&t=313s); [$1M biz for a stranger 19:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=1174s))
4. **Signs you're underpriced.** A 30–40% close rate means the price is too low; at 60%, double it. His team closed 12% in a $3.5M month, so don't plan on 20–30%. If you haven't raised prices in the last year you're almost certainly undercharging, and even a 20–30% bump (below ~$3M/month) unlocks hidden profit. Index to inflation: one owner hadn't raised prices in 6+ years while costs rose ~50% cumulatively. Set price for paid cold leads, not warm ones: they closed about 40% of organic calls and under 10% of paid. ([Raw $5M convo 46:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2815s); [1.8% close rate 9:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OAtocDg2Mqo&t=593s); [Profit-margin math 3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W0pUXPxvQyI&t=217s))
5. **Start low or start high? His advice changes with proof.** *Start low:* with no testimonials, charge about half until proof catches up. Start underpriced so cold traffic converts, and once it sells on calls, double, then double again. Always charge something, even when exploring, because free gives no signal; his first client put down a card for $24K. Early low-price clients produce the case studies, and the higher price that follows adds healthy friction. Start where you can reliably close 3–5 a month, then step up ~30% at a time ($12K → $18K → $36K), logging metrics at each step. *Start high:* "always test higher prices first; you can always lower," and copy richer competitors' pricing. In practice: price low until proof exists, then climb aggressively. ([$0–500K checklist 2:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AZC82IKCiVI&t=157s); [Every revenue level 7:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=465s); [$1M biz for a stranger 26:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=1569s))
6. **Price ceilings: client satisfaction and rep morale.** Customer satisfaction caps price but only becomes measurable past ~$100K/month; run standardized NPS on the whole base every 30 days instead of "going off vibes." They once raised prices until close rate fell to 6%, which was more profitable than 12%, but reps were getting about one deal a week and lost motivation, so they lowered the price. Transactional reps need wins weekly or more often. Know whether you're the highest or lowest price in your market. ([1.8% close rate 10:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OAtocDg2Mqo&t=623s); [Raw $5M convo 48:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2906s); [Wife Q&A 8:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_j_83cglBA&t=495s))
7. **Position at an extreme.** Be the cheapest or the most expensive, because the middle gets crushed, though new players with small audiences are safer in the middle. Below ~$100K/month you usually can't sell to the richest buyers without proof; the shortcut is borrowing proof from work at a leading competitor. Give away anything that costs nothing to deliver, and charge a premium for anything with marginal cost. A cold audience that thinks you're a nobody gives you limited pricing power, so high-ticket without high touch needs a strong personal brand. ([Raw $5M convo 19:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=1195s); [$1M biz for a stranger 29:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=1754s); [$1M biz for a stranger 30:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=1846s))
8. **When demand exceeds capacity, raise price (the Richard Mille model).** Raise price, say no more often and lift the minimum qualifying threshold rather than adding seats. Inner-circle ladder: $1.5K → $2K → $3K → $4K → $5K → $6K → $7K → $10K/month, raised every month or two because too many people bought. At $6K he still sold about 25 annual deals a month, and $10K/month on an annual commitment with vetting was the sweet spot. Announce scheduled increases (e.g. quarterly) and keep them; it's urgency and "great marketing." Higher price brings wealthier, more committed clients who get better results. ([$1M/mo offer 19:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1145s); [$1M/mo offer 28:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=1700s); [$1M biz for a stranger 33:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=2001s))
9. **Collect upfront; drop monthly cancel-anytime billing.** His SaaS-style monthly billing was a mistake for a service. Iman Gadzhi's advice: "charge as much upfront as possible and expect to only get paid that ever." Moving to 6-month terms with paid-upfront discounts took them from $1.3M to $3M/month in about 30 days, with no new headcount and no sales retraining (the first call just said "sounds good"). They got fewer buyers but far fewer refunds. Why it works: clients equate payments with progress. The second weekly charge (~$1,500) landed during onboarding before any visible result and caused rage and refunds. People pay $10K once more readily than $1K/month for 24 months, and nearly all 24-month payers leave early. Upfront cash also covers CAC in months 1–2 instead of 5–6. ([Iman Gadzhi call 4:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ocPw7KvY710&t=281s); [Every revenue level 26:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1618s); [$20K lesson 3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=183s))
10. **Set the term just above average happy-client lifetime.** Clients staying ~4–5 months → sign 6-month contracts (+20–30% topline). Don't jump to 12 months: it's harder to sell, and keeping people past the value creates review, chargeback and complaint problems. Make monthly unattractive and discount 3, 6 and 12 months paid upfront. His worked example is an exit-planning consultant at $6K/month whose clients left at month 5: a 6-month minimum at $36K paid monthly or $30K paid in full. Benchmark: a typical high-ticket business collects ~30% of total contract value in the first 30 days; Daniel collects ~85%. If you collect too much upfront, raise the price. *Tension:* his capped inner circle sells annual commitments with 3–4% annualised churn, and *5 Frameworks* endorses retainers. Long terms work where the client relationship is ongoing, not for front-end services. ([Every revenue level 30:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1834s); [Raw $5M convo 37:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2233s); [Scaling to $10M 1:24:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=5080s))
11. **Sell an outcome, not a time period.** Price outcome-based and upfront (e.g. a guaranteed million views, defined ad results within six months). Book analogy: nobody keeps paying for a book they've already read, so match payment to when value is delivered. ([$20K lesson 0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=0s); [$20K lesson 0:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=30s); [$20K lesson 4:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=274s))
12. **Payment ladder: present one option at a time, starting with pay-in-full.** (1) Pay in full by wire at 15% off (saves card fees and admin). (2) Half now, half in 1–4 weeks at 5% off, a decoy that makes paying in full look better. (3) First month as deposit, then weekly payments from day 28, with a setup fee, via in-house financing. (4) Third-party financing over a year or more. Move down only after each is declined. For budget objections, change the terms, not the price (same total, more closes). First isolate cash vs value doubt ("money aside, would this work for you?"), then ask "how much can you put down today?" ([100K sales calls 1:08:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=4109s); [$100K vs $1M offers 13:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=793s); [100K sales calls 1:16:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=4601s))
13. **Price financing so net cash is equal.** $10K by card at 3% nets $9,700; financing at 15% nets $8,500, so the financed price must be higher. Internal plans should cost a few thousand more in total (2 × $4,100 = $8,200 vs $7,000 paid in full), and the shortest internal plan is quarterly, to cover onboarding cost. Match financing term to program term: 24-month loans on 12-month programs meant ~50% of cash came from loans clients kept paying after coaching ended, which killed renewals and likely got them dropped by Affirm. Matching terms cut financing to 20–30%, then 10% of cash. Splitit (no interest for 12 months against the buyer's card limit) beats Klarna-type 20–30% fees. Present financing as an optional convenience with its own fee. B2B buy-now-pay-later handles budget objections; one sales manager got three new financing options added after budget losses dominated. ([Scaling to $10M 1:12:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=4348s); [Scaling to $10M 1:09:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=4195s); [64 tools 14:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sXQ0KLtz1kA&t=895s))
14. **Deferred pay-in-full.** Let reps convert a payment-plan buyer to paid-in-full within 30 days (e.g. $2,300 down on a quarterly plan, then "pay $4,900 now to lock in the discount"). Paying reps extra commission for these upgrades made no difference, because tiered pricing justifies paying in full on its own. ([Scaling to $10M 1:26:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=5172s))
15. **Discount discipline.** Discount the core offer only on Black Friday/Cyber Monday; otherwise only on re-sales and renewals. ([Scaling to $10M 45:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r6KhTStpbZc&t=2756s))
16. **Make price transparent, all-in and collected on the call.** People hate hidden or added costs far more than a higher price (all-inclusive hotel vs $20 minibar water). Pre-frame price and timeline in the application so nobody is surprised on the call (e.g. "$30K per video, ~1-month turnaround, does that fit?"). With skeptical prospects, give the price, and sometimes the pitch, in the first 5 minutes. Put contract and payment on one page (HubSpot Quotes), and take payment and signature on the call: more deals are lost to "I'll pay right after the call" than are won in follow-up. Use a processor built for high-ticket: Stripe held a percentage of revenue for ~3 months over voluntary refunds. ([Selling cold traffic 6:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=401s); [$1M biz for a stranger 6:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=373s); [100K sales calls 1:12:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=4356s))

> "Raising prices is almost always way easier than you think." ([Profit-margin math 5:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W0pUXPxvQyI&t=340s))
> "If I have a 30% or 40% close rate, this rather indicates that the price is too low" ([Raw $5M convo 47:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2876s))
> "Maximum value at the start, maximum payment at the start." ([$20K lesson 1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=61s))

**For G&L:**
- Sell fixed-scope, fixed-term programs collected upfront, never an open monthly retainer. Set each term just above the average time to the milestone: e.g. buy-side 6 months (average time to an accepted LOI, plus a month) and sell-side 9–12 months of exit-readiness. Treat success fees on closing as back-end upside and run the business on 30-day cash collected, not projected fees.
- Copy Daniel's exit-planning example directly for sell-side: monthly price ≈ 20% above paid-in-full (e.g. 6 × $6K = $36K monthly vs $30K paid in full). Collect ≥50% in the first 30 days; if you routinely collect ~85%, raise the price.
- Price sell-side above buy-side. A seller's outcome is a seven-figure exit, and they already expect to pay brokers a percentage. Buy-side should start where you can close 3–5 per month per closer, then step up ~30% at a time as LOI and closing case studies arrive. Offer founding-client pricing (~half) only until 5–10 documented deals exist.
- Payment ladder on calls, one option at a time: pay in full by wire at 15% off → 50/50 (balance after the first deal review) at 5% off → deposit plus monthly instalments with a setup fee → third-party financing over the program term only. Financed price = PIF + fees, and the minimum internal plan is quarterly.
- Run price tests on paid cold leads: double the fee for a week. If fewer than half drop off, keep it. If qualified-call close rate exceeds ~30%, raise again. Stop when closers fall below about one win a week.
- Announce dated quarterly fee increases in emails and on the VSL page, and keep them.
- Send one engagement-letter-plus-payment link on the call and take the wire screenshot or card before hanging up. Set up a high-ticket-friendly processor before scaling.
- Disclose every third-party cost (legal, QoE, lender and escrow fees) in the application pre-frame and on the call, so there are no surprises later.

### A3. Guarantees & trials

1. **They are two different tools; don't confuse them.** A conditional *outcome guarantee* is a marketing tool: it buys cheaper leads (more volume for the same spend), not higher close rates. A *7-day money-back trial / refundable deposit* is a sales tool that beats hesitation. On calls the trial raised top-line revenue 30%, a trust guarantee added "a couple of million dollars a month," and net of refunds and delivery the trial still adds hundreds of thousands a month. Diagnose the right one or you'll blame the wrong problem. ([$3M in one month 11:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uci8AF7uU9A&t=680s); [$720K emails 3:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SGeD3rpjlBA&t=183s); [Guarantees suck 24:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1449s))
2. **Cold traffic needs an outcome guarantee.** The main cold objection is "sounds great if it works." Guarantee the specific outcome they want, in writing, or they don't pay. Alternatives: pay-per-lead, profit share, pay on delivery. The aggressive scaling path usually requires a quantified outcome or guarantee, and it should be built for a fast one-call yes ("work out the specifics after they're a client"). Keep conditions simple: pay on time and participate. ([$100K vs $1M offers 15:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=915s); [$10K vs $1M ads 16:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2clMOGeFzJQ&t=984s); [$1M biz for a stranger 11:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=714s))
3. **Design it from your own outcome data so it makes you money.** Guarantees exist because businesses that understand their outcome data better than customers do earn more with one. Build conditions from hundreds of client results so failure is nearly impossible, aim for ~80% of clients never claiming, and model refund exposure (cash upfront vs payment plans, margins) before launching. The original "1M views in 1 month, post 15 videos" came from hand-picked first clients who all hit it. For the mass market the timeline stretched 1 → 2 → 3 → 6 months. Guarantee something modest and very achievable: today it needs only ~5,500 views/video, while the average client is at ~15.5M views by month 3. ([Raw $5M convo 34:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2050s); [Guarantees suck 14:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=895s); [Guarantees suck 19:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1172s))
4. **Believability beats size.** Raising the guarantee from 1M to 10M views sounded like a scam to less sophisticated owners. It lowered CTR and trust and cut sales, and a split test confirmed the 1M wording won. Match the guarantee to what the customer believes. A guarantee that sounds like a cheap gimmick attracts worse clients; a credible one supports a higher price. ([Guarantees suck 16:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=987s); [Raw $5M convo 36:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=2172s))
5. **Condition rules.** (a) Always include "payments on time": missed payments on year-long high-ticket contracts can hit 40–60%, and it filters out cash-strapped clients, the hardest to get results for. (b) Require attendance as a rolling percentage (e.g. 75% of weekly strategy calls), not a lump count that can be gamed at the end, and don't call them "Q&A sessions." (c) Fold thresholds and timelines into the description ("if within 180 days you haven't signed 10 clients") rather than listing hoops; one program's 10 conditions killed its calls. (d) Each condition should be something the client wants anyway, should enforce consistency, or should let you control quality. Viral Coach's current set: pay on time; post 30 videos/month for 6 consecutive months on all major platforms; edited by a Viral Coach–trained editor. (e) Add one catch-all nullifier for the act that sabotages results (e.g. buying followers). ([Guarantees suck 3:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=215s); [Guarantees suck 10:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=650s); [Guarantees suck 17:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1048s))
6. **Conditions to cut, and the ethics line.** Cut: every call recorded or tracked (one glitch disqualifies); offer must be audited or changed by your team (a "sales call killer"); complete all modules; email us by day 120; impossible volumes (1,300 cold emails/day, 500 DMs/day). The unethical version hides conditions almost nobody meets. Example: a $40K+, 1-year program guaranteeing $150K annualised revenue required 90% of ~568 calls (511, about 43/month, 2+ hours per business day) and reportedly never paid out. Ask "how many people actually redeem this?" A simpler, looser guarantee makes more money overall because it converts skeptics who see through gotchas. ([Guarantees suck 12:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=742s); [Guarantees suck 6:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=368s); [Guarantees suck 14:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=865s))
7. **Bridge intermediate metrics.** A guarantee on views invites "views don't pay bills," so the VSL ties views to revenue immediately. ([$10M VSL 1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_3-WQstsN5I&t=61s))
8. **Placement.** VSL hook = promise plus proof via the guarantee ("a million views or you don't pay") plus a visual open loop, backed by a full written refund, with the CTA "apply to see if you qualify." Restate it in every email PS. Send guarantee-angle ads to a dedicated guarantee funnel. Keep result and guarantee claims out of ad headlines (ban risk; put them in the VSL or lower on the page) and out of evergreen reused post IDs, since copy can't be edited later. ([Content flywheel 15:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xvOnEA48xZY&t=928s); [$720K emails 2:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SGeD3rpjlBA&t=153s); [Meta Ads Blueprint 49:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eEiyrvM1nB8&t=2969s))
9. **7-day trial mechanics.** The prospect puts down a small deposit and attends one call in the first 7 days, with a full no-questions refund, offered on this call only. Most calls are lost to hesitation, not to a no (the "I'll start next week" stall cost them 30–40% of deals). Script: "You're busy; next week I won't have anything new and you won't have better information. Let our team prove it; if you don't like it, tell them and get a full refund." Declining a risk-free option becomes the active decision to cross you off, so option-keepers default to trying, and inertia carries them through onboarding. It suits problem-aware, solution-unaware buyers who can't judge a solution from a pitch (borrowed from SaaS). It turns "I need to learn more before I buy" into "I buy to learn more." ([Guarantees suck 20:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1233s); [Selling cold traffic 4:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fs_1ndiqpHU&t=279s); [Every revenue level 36:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2172s))
10. **The trial answers most objections.** Too expensive: "just a deposit; see the ROI in a week." Partner or spouse: "bring them to the first meeting." Fit: "that's what week one is for; talk to the account manager who'll do the work." Timing: "the 7-day clock doesn't start until you're ready; a deposit holds your spot." Think about it: "that's exactly why we offer a week, so you have something real to think about." Value doubt: "you can't know the value from the outside; our team will work free for 7 days." Window shoppers: any stated criterion leads to "start with the guarantee," and no answer is a hard DQ. Make it the default for undecided prospects. ([Guarantees suck 22:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1354s); [5 lessons 5:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J0YiARB-zXY&t=338s); [100K sales calls 56:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=3361s))
11. **Refunds only on a recorded call with the account manager.** Reps began using the trial as a crutch for every objection, so clients arrived unsold, and many took the overdelivered week-one roadmap and left to DIY. The fix that took them from $3M to $5M: refunds only by telling your account manager on the onboarding call, not by emailing support (Planet Fitness logic). Refunds stopped. ([Every revenue level 38:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2295s); [Disappointment of $5M 1:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=94s))
12. **The first 7 days decide whether the trial pays.** At $3–4M, understanding why some clients stayed and others refunded added almost $2M gross in one month. A same-day onboarding change cut refunds from ~40% to ~5% overnight (retained revenue ~$2M → nearly $3M/month). Separately, the 7-day refund rate fell from ~20% to 10% to 8.8% over six months. Cautionary tale: $5M in contract value closed in one month, fulfillment couldn't deliver inside the window, and almost $2M was refunded. Instrument the week. The trial backfires if week-one delivery is weak or sales overpromised, and you end up doing free work. ([$5M in one month 3:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jm14bVak6rA&t=215s); [100-person team 25:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=1545s); [100K sales calls 1:20:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=4816s))
13. **Use the trial to police overselling.** It doubles as a fail-safe: oversold clients refund and the rep earns nothing. Reps lose commission on refunds and are fined if too many clients back out. It is conditional, runs from the payment date, is offered only to some prospects, and works because onboarding delivers value within minutes. Watch processor risk: Stripe held a reserve because of voluntary refunds. ([$20K/mo reps 9:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oLR7G2L6bDg&t=586s); [$3M funnel 10:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o1i_mFCOJZI&t=624s); [$3M funnel 22:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o1i_mFCOJZI&t=1336s))
14. **Where he contradicts himself.** (a) He says testimonials "beat bold guarantees and promises almost every time" in testing, yet his VSL hook and top ad angle is the guarantee. Lead with proof, and use the guarantee as the hook and reassurance. (b) In *Guarantees suck* he says guarantee something modest and over-deliver; in the *Raw $5M convo* he says "don't under-promise and over-deliver; promise accurately, charge for it and deliver accurately." Reconcile by keeping the *guarantee floor* modest and the *sales promise* accurate. ([$116K/day landing page 9:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w5bYv2CBDZE&t=557s); [Guarantees suck 19:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1172s); [Raw $5M convo 52:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CLJ9AnFuSZk&t=3151s))

> "Hesitation, left alone, almost always unfortunately defaults to a lost sale." ([Guarantees suck 21:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1263s))
> "One removes the fear of starting, the other removes the fear of failing." ([Guarantees suck 25:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1510s))
> "From 'I can't buy, I need to learn more first' to 'if I want to learn more, I need to buy first.'" ([$100K vs $1M offers 16:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=976s))

**For G&L:**
- **Buy-side conditional outcome guarantee** (milestone, not "$0 down"): "If you haven't had an LOI accepted on a seller-financed business inside your buy-box within 12 months, we keep working with you at no further fee until you do." Conditions (max 3): payments on time; ≥75% attendance at weekly deal calls (rolling); a minimum weekly owner-outreach or NDA count. Nullifier: going around the advisor to the seller, or misstating available capital.
- **Sell-side guarantee on readiness, not price** (the sale price depends on the market): "Market-ready in 9 months (CIM, clean 3-year financials, valuation, buyer list) or we work free until it is," conditional on delivering financials by agreed dates and on-time payments. Never guarantee a multiple or a closing date.
- Don't launch "buy a business with no money down, guaranteed." It sounds too good to be true, like 10M views. Split-test "accepted LOI in 12 months" against "X vetted, financeable deals presented in 90 days," and set the bar from the first 10–20 clients' actual results so ~80% never claim.
- **7-day refundable deposit, on this call only**, to beat "let me think about it" and "I need to ask my spouse/accountant." Invite the partner to the kickoff. Week-one deliverables must be tangible: buyers get buy-box, first target list and deal-finance plan; sellers get a valuation snapshot, exit-readiness score and top 3 EBITDA levers.
- Refunds only on a recorded call with the assigned advisor, never by email. Claw back closer commission on refunds and track refund rate by rep and by weekly cohort as a headline KPI.
- Don't push ad spend past what advisors can onboard inside the 7-day window. A backlog becomes refunds (the ~$2M lesson).
- Put the guarantee in the VSL, the email PS and a dedicated guarantee funnel, never in the Meta headline. Record every call so it's clear what was promised about financing.

### A4. Fulfillment, delivery & client results

1. **Fulfillment is the real growth ceiling.** A profitable $100–500K/month business can usually reach $1M just by spending more on ads; the bottleneck is nuanced, non-plug-and-play fulfillment. Test: "Could you double sales right now and have fulfillment be okay?" Bad fulfillment kills a growing business faster than slow sales, because commission is already paid and you carry refund, chargeback and bad-review risk. A $10M goal implies ~3× ad spend → ~2× reps → more CS and fulfillment staff, or the revenue turns into refunds. ([Every revenue level 14:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=868s); [100-person team 10:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=618s); [$3M month Q&A 3:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YkEuo4P6vKs&t=218s))
2. **Staff delivery ahead of the push.** Around $1M/month the next five hires were account managers and associate AMs, and fulfillment doubled from 7 to 14 people. Going from $3M to $5M needed about 4 more reps and 8 more AMs. While stuck at $1.3M for 5–6 months he built a queue of trained AMs with SOPs, so nothing broke when growth came. Hire in alternating waves (sales, then fulfillment, then legal/ops). ([$3M team 5:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ds1rAvgIzrA&t=306s); [$3M team 11:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ds1rAvgIzrA&t=681s); [Every revenue level 33:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2017s))
3. **Onboard before the sales call ends.** Payment by wire screenshot or card link; a card triggers automatic onboarding. The client gets DocuSign, a welcome page with an intake survey, joins Slack and books the AM onboarding call, all within 5–10 minutes. Handoff: an automatic ping, ops tags the associate AM and AM, the rep records a ~1-minute voice memo summarizing the call and suggesting the right AM, the AM sends a voice-note intro within the hour (often within 5 minutes), and the onboarding call happens within 24–48 hours. Give immediate access (Christmas-tree analogy: people pay for speed). Start urgent onboarding tasks the same day, and when you can't, surface blockers; a "20–30 minute" upload of ~700 creatives took 5+ hours. ([$3M funnel 23:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o1i_mFCOJZI&t=1397s); [$3M funnel 23:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o1i_mFCOJZI&t=1427s); [100K sales calls 1:13:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=4387s))
4. **Front-load visible value.** During the 7-day window the team immediately builds a customised program, so by day 7 the client sees progress and stopping feels like the disruption. The onboarding call (a hyper-focused strategy built with automations and market research, delivered by a niche-experienced AM) is what makes people stick. Sales picks the AM by industry expertise and personality, and showing the prospect who's "on the other side of the fence" closes them even if they don't love the rep. ([Guarantees suck 22:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OsktcdNDATU&t=1323s); [Every revenue level 40:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2447s); [Every revenue level 42:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=2540s))
5. **Set expectations bluntly and keep the promise consistent.** At onboarding, say the work is hard, takes months and needs effort, and that anyone expecting quick, effortless results should ask for a refund now. If the founder is the face but doesn't deliver, sell "my method/system" (or the founder as spokesperson), never "I'll personally coach you," then hand off to a CSM. The clearer the expectation before purchase and the more complete the product, the less the founder is needed. Record every call (Fathom) to settle "you promised X" disputes by timestamp. ([$36K training 3:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=185s); [$3M AMA 2:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rO6lGSXn42E&t=158s); [64 tools 7:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sXQ0KLtz1kA&t=434s))
6. **Sell accountability, not information.** Clients hire you for accountability (Planet Fitness, a personal trainer). Hold them to what they said they wanted through an assigned AM and calls. Keep training short: a brief base course plus an AM who answers their specific situation. Low price means low skin in the game; as with SaaS onboarding, the #1 job is getting the client to use the product early, because users stick. DFY gets near-100% activation because the service happens whether or not the client "does the work." ([Disappointment of $5M 2:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-l4SKfxstC4&t=160s); [$36K training 1:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hp328yUV6O8&t=61s); [$1M biz for a stranger 20:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eWMz7IfiHbE&t=1234s))
7. **Make results visible and owned.** Clients often can't recognize a good result, so deliver it, show them why it's good, wrap it in service, and plan the support needed in month 1 vs month 6. Record baselines in an intake survey (followers, views, posting frequency, CAC, ad spend vs revenue); a stated goal of "one 20K-view video" turns a later 1.5M-view video into a story of beating expectations. Own attribution or lose credit and renewals: clients judge the last 30 days of cost against the last 30 days of results. AMs ask which deals the client closed recently and trace each to an ad or organic touch. ([Every revenue level 16:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=993s); [Case studies make millions 13:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=808s); [Case studies make millions 10:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=651s))
8. **Structure: senior specialist up front, production behind.** Hospital model: a specialist "doctor" spends brief, high-leverage time diagnosing and prescribing, and nurses and assistants execute, unlike agencies that hand clients to a minimum-wage generalist. Big Four, law and investment-banking firms work the same way: a sharp client-facing lead backed by a large back office. Use the McDonald's model and fragment the work (billing, late payments and legal ops; tech; production; posting) so the CSM mostly talks with clients and top talent isn't "the janitor." Promote an internal AM to CXO to own client experience. ([Easy businesses win 3:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i4mWmsC0mTA&t=216s); [100-person team 12:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=741s); [$3M AMA 10:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rO6lGSXn42E&t=622s))
9. **Pay client-facing staff well and tie bonuses to proof.** Don't save money with cheap nearshore VAs in client-facing roles. Pay six-figure-plus OTEs to AMs in the client's own country, often smarter than the client; retention pays back more than the savings. AM pay is a base per client plus milestone bonuses for referrals, retention/LTV, case studies, testimonials and reviews. Part-time AMs have never worked, and keep one AM visibly earning "crazy money." Production roles stay salaried and are judged on fewer revisions. Rewriting a ~50-page AM handbook, recording tutorials and rebuilding comp cut fulfillment cost to a third while paying top performers more. ([100-person team 10:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=649s); [100-person team 13:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=803s); [I failed 17:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUgSDt1Kp4g&t=1026s))
10. **Fulfillment KPIs and cost targets.** Track refunds, support tickets, client results and frequency of client communication (a leading indicator), plus 30-day NPS. Track payment status (delinquent, approved vs unapproved pause, ghosting) where AMs can see it. Delivery cost targets: under 10% of gross at $1M/month, 5% or less at a few million, ~0.5–1% at the largest firms. Fix fulfillment complexity mostly by raising prices, and move upmarket only when price can't rise further. ([5 lessons 1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=J0YiARB-zXY&t=92s); [1M/mo lesson 7:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y8WB72fKX1Q&t=461s); [64 tools 3:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sXQ0KLtz1kA&t=185s))
11. **Scale 1:1 delivery with documentation.** Add a weekly group call once 1:1s and DMs repeat the same questions. Then document everything (SOPs, a 180+ call library, an AI clone) so only ~20% of members book 1:1s (30–40% at peak). Curate the group: when two people complain about one member, that member is removed and refunded. Specialisation compounds, because one service for one client type gets faster with every client. ([$1M/mo offer 2:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=156s); [$1M/mo offer 4:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AfvS5Tkhbis&t=247s); [Best version overnight 4:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VLNCU_ZM0Gk&t=266s))
12. **Cold-traffic clients are harder.** Organic clients trust you, and a free extra call smooths issues. Cold-traffic clients need a more complex ops and support team and brutal attention to detail, because they're far less forgiving. Refund rates track the market's demographics more than the product, so they avoid certain segments. ([Organic vs paid 8:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Hk0k66Pc5Dk&t=490s); [Lessons to $3M 50:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kkqcPEj0-po&t=3031s))
13. **Churn comes from the billing model and from shallow integration.** As with personal training, clients get most of the value in the first weeks and then want to cut out the middleman, so that churn comes from the billing model, not service quality. Owning a large share of the client's critical infrastructure makes them sticky. Back-end delivery tailored to each client can't be copied by watching your front end. Have strong IP and contract lawyers, because some clients take the work and charge back. ([$20K lesson 1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KkLU1WjvZxM&t=92s); [$100K vs $1M offers 19:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=29qOh8ZnF0k&t=1160s); [Easy businesses win 6:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i4mWmsC0mTA&t=401s))

> "They attribute value and the price that they pay to the amount of progress that they make." ([Every revenue level 28:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AmXrrw8OGa0&t=1739s))
> "Someone to get the clients, someone to keep the clients, and then someone to keep the other two people out of jail." ([100-person team 0:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5YaXZkjo0V0&t=0s))

**For G&L:**
- Onboard on the call: engagement letter, payment and intake form in one link. Buyer intake: liquid capital, buy-box, industry experience, deal size, timeline. Seller intake: 3 years of financials, EBITDA, owner dependency, last valuation. Then a private client channel, an advisor voice-note intro within the hour, and a kickoff within 24–48 hours.
- Engineer week one around a visible deliverable. Buyers: signed buy-box, first 20–30 off-market targets, financing plan (vendor note + bank/SBA mix). Sellers: valuation snapshot, exit-readiness score, top 3 EBITDA levers (start with the price/margin fix).
- Use the hospital/IB structure: a senior M&A advisor diagnoses structure and valuation and leads negotiations; analysts and deal coordinators run sourcing, modelling, data rooms and follow-ups; QoE, legal and tax are contracted to partner firms. Keep client-facing advisors credible to 55+ owners and well paid.
- Deliver accountability, not content: weekly deal-flow targets (owner conversations, NDAs, LOIs) for buyers, monthly readiness milestones for sellers, and a monthly scorecard against the intake baseline. Run 30-day NPS once volume allows.
- Before raising ad spend, forecast advisor capacity (clients per advisor) and apply the test "could we onboard double next month?"
- Pay advisors a base per client plus bonuses for accepted LOIs and closings, testimonials or case-study releases, and referrals.
- Set realistic timelines at onboarding (first LOI usually takes months; exits run 2–3 years of prep) and record every call, especially anything said about financing terms.
- Log refund and cancellation reasons per cohort, and fix onboarding the same week a pattern appears.

### A5. Case studies & proof

1. **Proof is the most valuable asset.** Prospects pay based on what they believe you can do for them. In testing, a flood of testimonials beat bold guarantees almost every time. Hundreds of reviews and case studies cut the sales cycle from 1+ week to a couple of days and nearly doubled close rate in 8 months. With no client results yet, use your own result or one from a previous company, or borrow proof from work at a leading competitor. ([Case studies make millions 0:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=31s); [$116K/day landing page 9:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w5bYv2CBDZE&t=557s); [$3M in one month 12:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uci8AF7uU9A&t=741s))
2. **Map 3–5 value buckets first.** Viral Coach's four are reach (views/followers), time efficiency, authority (looking professional, press) and direct business impact (leads, ROAS, revenue). Each new win drops into one or two buckets. ([Case studies make millions 1:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=93s); [Case studies make millions 2:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=155s))
3. **Package each result four ways** so studies don't sound identical: a raw number (10M views), a percentage (+900%), a multiple (100×, which hits harder than "+10,000%"), or tied to a timeframe (10M views in 30 days, his favourite). One client reads as "made $100K", "+60% revenue", "10× from ads" and "4× the business in 6 months." ([Case studies make millions 4:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=248s); [Case studies make millions 5:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=340s))
4. **Collect wins continuously, not at the end.** Run a company-wide #client-wins channel with posts every day, weekends included; ex-clients ghost, so collecting afterwards is nearly impossible. Pull each win as a thread: one 1.7M-view post led to a full-account review showing 4 posts and 22K views in the 90 days before vs 362 posts (+9,000%), 5M+ views (190×) and 50K followers in 90 days with them. Record baselines at intake. Tie AM bonuses to case studies, testimonials and reviews. Collect reviews on Clutch, G2 and Trustpilot, and employee reviews on Glassdoor. ([Case studies make millions 6:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=372s); [Case studies make millions 9:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=556s); [64 tools 11:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sXQ0KLtz1kA&t=711s))
5. **One-slide format with five elements:** (1) client name and face, (2) what they do, for pattern-matching, (3) a visual before/after, (4) one scroll-stopping headline result, (5) supporting stats as "free dessert." Simpler converts better. Don't explain the strategy: early 15-minute walkthroughs went unwatched or taught prospects to DIY. When asked how, the answer is "pay me and I'll show you." ([Case studies make millions 15:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=903s); [Case studies make millions 17:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=1027s))
6. **Match proof to the problem, not the industry.** "Show me a case study exactly like me" is a moving target: one prospect had every escalating condition met and still ghosted after 3 months. Better responses: for new founders, "let's make you the first in your space" (avoid this with established owners); otherwise show the closest case and point out the *differences* so the prospect argues the similarities. One client (Jared: 200M views, 427K followers) covers four pains: 40 → 4 hours/month on content, replacing $60K/month of ads, most-followed plumber, revenue. Build a ~30-slide deck segmented by prospect type, send it pre-call and know it "like a Vegas dealer." Setters dig into vague application answers so the closer brings matching studies. ([$5M objections 6:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=398s); [100K sales calls 1:02:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MPhvqxVGzkw&t=3735s); [Case studies make millions 20:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxtXMHvdArs&t=1244s))
7. **Weave proof through the call.** Drop a case study the moment a pain shows up, so the prospect has 5–10 reasons by the time the price comes. Don't save proof for a big reveal, since B2B owners will say "just tell me the price." Never hand out client contacts: of 15 prospects who said a client call would make them "100% in," only 1 signed. Line: "I can't give you my clients' contact info, but I promise I won't give yours out either." For "get me results first, then I'll pay," point to 1,000+ clients and five-star reviews, then reframe from "does this work?" to "will it work for me?" ([Close $1M/mo as a rep 9:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lxoxvG4bj90&t=590s); [$5M objections 2:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=121s); [$5M objections 3:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qv1VrleLB08&t=215s))
8. **Email every win to the whole list.** Case-study emails are their 5th–9th most profitable. Format: before (344 followers) → after 90 days (100K followers, 10M views in 30 days) → screenshots → "click here to work with us" → PS with the guarantee → PPS with a wall of reviews.
